Self-organized regime switching in null-recurrent dynamics
[, [, [, [

Abstract
Based on discrete observations X 0 , X Δ , … , X n ​ Δ X_{0},X_{\Delta},\dots,X_{n\Delta} for Δ = n − γ \Delta=n^{-\gamma} with γ ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \gamma\in[0,1) of the null-recurrent dynamic d ​ X t = σ ⁡ ( X t ) ​ d ​ W t dX_{t}=\sigma(X_{t})dW_{t} with a Brownian motion W W and σ ( x ) = α 𝟙 { x < ρ } + β 𝟙 { x ≥ ρ } \sigma(x)=\alpha\mathbbm{1}\{x<\rho\}+\beta\mathbbm{1}\{x\geq\rho\} , we derive rate of convergence and limiting distribution of the profile MLE for ρ \rho . This includes low-frequency asymptotics ( γ = 0 \gamma=0 ) for which the observations form a null-recurrent Markov chain. The derived non-standard limit is the argsup over a doubly stochastic drifted Poisson process explicitly involving the local time of oscillating Brownian motion. Its dependence on ρ \rho as well as the unknown volatility levels α \alpha and β \beta is shown to be continuous w.r.t. the topology of weak convergence, enabling statistical inference. Whereas this limit is independent of the sampling frequency, the profile MLE’s rate of convergence equals n − ( 1 + γ ) / 2 n^{-(1+\gamma)/2} and is proven to be minimax optimal. The surprising idea of the proof of the limit theorem is to relate the long-term behavior of the null-recurrent Markov chain to the infill asymptotics on a fixed time interval. Indeed, in the very special case that ( X t ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t})_{t\geq 0} is started in the true parameter X 0 = ρ 0 X_{0}=\rho_{0} , the process ( X t − ρ 0 ) t ≥ 0 (X_{t}-\rho_{0})_{t\geq 0} is shown to possess a desirable distributional self-similarity. On basis of the strong Markov property, the artificial constallation of starting in ρ 0 \rho_{0} is finally overcome by a coupling argument.



A]Johannes Brutsche,
A]Sebastian Hahn
and A]Angelika Rohde

1
Introduction and preliminaries

While Brownian motion originally describes random motion of particles suspended in a medium, oscillating Brownian motion as introduced in
Keilson and Wellner 1978
naturally arises in the description of diffusive motion in porous or highly inhomogeneous media. Therefore, it serves as a toy model for change-point phenomena with structural breaks not depending on time but purely on state of the process itself. Apart from natural sciences, self-organized regime switching phenomena are also observed in economics, see for example
Yuan and Yau 2026
and references therein. Figure
1
contains an example of stock prices, illustrating a typical sample path with an abrupt change at the threshold
\rho
between two different volatility levels.

Fig 1:
Sotheby’s (BID) stock prices, 1988-2012, from
Yahoo!Finance
. The process switches its volatility regime at a threshold
\rho
of approximately
22
.

In mathematical terms, oscillating Brownian motion is a Markov process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
solving the homogeneous stochastic differential equation

\displaystyle dX_{t}=\sigma_{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(X_{t})dW_{t},\qquad X_{0}=x_{0}\in\mathbb{R},

(1.1)

where
W
is a standard Brownian motion and for different (but arbitrary) numbers
\alpha,\beta\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
, the diffusion coefficient is given by

\displaystyle\sigma_{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x):=\begin{cases}\alpha,&\textrm{ if }x<\rho,\\
\beta,&\textrm{ if }x\geq\rho.\end{cases}

(1.2)

As shown in
Le Gall 1984
, the SDE (
1.1
) possesses a unique strong solution. This solution is a strong Markov process, cf.
Keilson and Wellner 1978
, who also identified the transition density
p_{t}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
from state
x
to state
y
within time
t
for
\rho=0
. It is given for general
\rho
in (
2.1
) and one reads off that it is
not
continuous in the parameter
\rho
of interest and satisfies

p_{t}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=p_{t}^{0,\alpha,\beta}(x-\rho,y-\rho),

which will play a major role for this article. Together with the explicit density of the passage time distribution of
X
in Theorem
2
in
Keilson and Wellner 1978
for
\rho=0
, the process
X
is seen to be null-recurrent. Especially, in contrast to stationary processes, an invariant probability distribution does not exist and the classical ergodic theorem is not available.

Whereas the threshold
\rho
is observable via quadratic variation given any finite continuous trajectory
(X_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
that crosses
\rho
, its identification is a statistical problem on the basis of discrete observations

X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\ \ k=0,1,\dots,n,

with
\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}=n^{-\gamma}
for
\gamma\in[0,1]
. For the so-called infill asymptotics corresponding to
\gamma=1
, the time horizon
n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}
is bounded, whereas for all
\gamma\in[0,1)
it tends to infinity (see Figure
2
).

x
\tfrac{1}{n}
\tfrac{2}{n}
\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
\tfrac{2}{\sqrt{n}}
0
1
2
3
4
5

Fig 2:
An illustration of different observation schemes. The red lines correspond to
\gamma=1
, the infill asympotics, where no point after
T=1
is observed. The blue dashes correspond to the low frequency observation scheme
\gamma=0
. The green ones correspond to
\gamma=1/2
, where the observations are more dense with growing
n
, but also the observation window increases.

Likewise, the only observation scheme for which the recurrence structure is irrelevant is the infill asymptotics (the red scheme in Figure
2
). Here, the maximum likelihood estimator (MLE) for
\rho
has been studied in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
when the two volatility levels
\alpha
and
\beta
are known to the statistician. All other scenarios
\gamma\in[0,1)
are still open and content of this article, where we derive rate of convergence and limiting distribution of the profile MLE for
\rho
as
n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rightarrow\infty
(cf. Theorem
2.1
). Compared to the MLE, the profile MLE is even applicable when the volatility levels
\alpha
and
\beta
are unknown. Our study includes the particular case that the observations form a fixed null-recurrent Markov chain (
\gamma=0
). Generally speaking, for all
\gamma\in[0,1)
, we are facing two severe challenges at once:

•

the discontinuity of the likelihood function in the parameter of interest and

•

the missing law of large numbers due to the null-recurrence of the dynamics.

The more surprising it is that in our case, the long-run asymptotics (
\gamma\in[0,1)
) can be traced back to the infill asymptotics (
\gamma=1
). Indeed, just when
X_{0}=\rho_{0}
, the process
(X_{t}-\rho_{0})_{t\geq 0}
is discovered to possess a distributional self-similarity under the true parameter
\rho_{0}
. As a consequence, the distribution of the likelihood function can be expressed in terms of the infill observation scheme. By the strong Markov property and a coupling argument this idea is expanded to arbitrary starting points. The design of this proof strategy is the core of this article. Moreover, this route of proof illuminates the occurence of the local time of an independent oscillating Brownian motion in
\rho_{0}
in the intensity of the profile MLE’s doubly stochastic Poisson-type limit which is completely incomprehensible from a long-run asymptotic’s perspective. Inter alia, this non-standard limit is caused by the discontinuity of the likelihood function in the threshold parameter
\rho
.

We highlight that the limit distribution does not depend on the value of
\gamma
in the observation scheme, whereas its convergence rate is identified as
n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}
and proven to be minimax optimal. Note that the derived
\sqrt{n}
-consistency in case
\gamma=0
does
not
correspond to the classical parametric rate in regular models as the likelihood function is discontinuous in the parameter
\rho
. Instead, it reflects the difficulty inherent to null-recurrent dynamics as compared to stationary time series settings (cf.
Chan 1993
,
Yuan and Yau 2026
) and is actually fast, see also
Delattre and
Hoffmann 2002
. Finally, we show that the doubly stochastic Poisson-type limit depends continuously on
\alpha,\beta
and
\rho
in the topology of weak convergence (cf. Theorem
2.2
), enabling the construction of asymptotic confidence intervals for the threshold
\rho
.

To the best of our knowledge, the various literature on estimating a threshold in regime switching models in long-term asymptotics rely on ergodic properties so far. The contributions
Kutoyants 2012
,
Su and Chan 2015
,
Su and Chan 2017
, and
Mazzonetto and
Pigato 2024
cover inference in ergodic threshold diffusions as the time horizon of the observed trajectories goes to infinity. The typical limit of such change-points is given as the argmax of a drifted two-sided Brownian motion, see for example Theorem
4
in
Su and Chan 2015
. This type of limit also occurs in
Reiß, Strauch and
Trottner 2026
in context of change-point estimation for the stochastic heat equation. A limit involving compound Poisson processes arises in
Chan 1993
in the context of threshold autoregressive time series models.
Yuan and Yau 2026
study an autoregressive model in which the regime-switching is described by a bivariate threshold variable based on minimizing the minimum description length. The respective objective function is not càdlàg and the limiting distribution theory is developed in a space of pure jump processes. In contrast to our model, both time series contributions work under the assumption of existence of an invariant measure.

The article is organized as follows: In Section
2
we present our main result on the distributional limit of the profile MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}
together with the statistical application including important properties of the limit and a simulation study. The proofs extend over Sections
3
-
7
with some technical details deferred to the Supplementary material (containing Appendices A-F).

2
Main results

The transition density
p_{t}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
for the process solving (
1.1
) is explicitly given as

\displaystyle p_{t}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}\alpha}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2t\alpha^{2}}\right)-\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-2\rho+x)^{2}}{2t\alpha^{2}}\right)\right]&\textrm{ for }x<\rho,y\leq\rho,\\
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}\beta}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{(y-x)^{2}}{2t\beta^{2}}\right)+\frac{\alpha-\beta}{\alpha+\beta}\exp\left(-\frac{(y-2\rho+x)^{2}}{2t\beta^{2}}\right)\right]&\textrm{ for }x\geq\rho,y>\rho,\\
\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta}\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t}\left(\frac{y-\rho}{\beta}-\frac{x-\rho}{\alpha}\right)^{2}\right)&\textrm{ for }x<\rho<y,\\
\frac{2}{\alpha+\beta}\frac{\beta}{\alpha}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi t}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2t}\left(\frac{y-\rho}{\alpha}-\frac{x-\rho}{\beta}\right)^{2}\right)&\textrm{ for }y\leq\rho\leq x.\\
\end{cases}

(2.1)

Indeed, by Proposition
4.2
in
Blanchard, Röckner and
Russo 2010
the transition density is given as the unique solution of the Kolmogorov forward (or Fokker–Planck) equation in a distributional sense, i.e. it satisfies

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}p_{t}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)\varphi(y)dy=\varphi(x)+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{\rho}(y)^{2}p_{s}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)\varphi^{\prime\prime}(y)dyds

for every
t\in[0,T]
and any
\varphi\in\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\mathcal{S}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the Schwartz space of rapidly decreasing functions on
\mathbb{R}
. One easily verifies that (
2.1
) satisfies this equation.
By the Markovian structure, the likelihood function for the observations
(X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})_{k=1,\dots,n}
factorizes into a product of transition densities such that the log-likelihood function is given as

\displaystyle L_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}(X_{0},X_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\dots,X_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}};\rho):=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})\right).

(2.2)

From the explicit transition density in (
2.1
) it is read off that for continuous functions
f_{k}
, the likelihood function can be written as

\sum_{k=0}^{n+1}f_{k}(\rho,\alpha,\beta)\mathbbm{1}_{I_{k}},

with
I_{0}=\{-\infty<\rho<X_{0}:n\}
,
\{X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}:n}\leq\rho<\infty)\}
and
I_{k}=\{X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}:n}\leq\rho<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}:n}\}
for
k=1,\dots,n
, where
(X_{0:n},X_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}:n},\dots,X_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}:n})
denoting the order statistic of the observations. For given
(\alpha,\beta)
this function is not continuous, but càdlàg in the parameter
\rho
. Let
f:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
be any function of the form
f(x,y)=\sum_{m=1}^{N}f_{k}(x,y)\mathbbm{1}_{\{x_{m-1}\leq x<x_{m}\}}
with
f_{k}:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
being continuous. Then we define

\underset{x\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }f(x)=\left\{(x_{1},x_{2})\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}:\ \max\left\{\lim_{u\nearrow x_{1}}f(u,x_{2}),f(x_{1},x_{2})\right\}=\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}_{>0}^{2}}f(z)\right\}

and write
\arg\sup
if
\mathrm{Argsup}
is a singleton. Note that
\mathrm{Argsup}
is always a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{3}
but may be empty. Moreover, the special case of càdlàg functions
g:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is part of this definition by considering
f(x_{1},x_{2},x_{3})=g(x_{1})
. We finally define the profile MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}
to be the first component of some measurably selected representative of

\displaystyle\underset{\rho\in\mathbb{R},\alpha,\beta>0}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ L_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}\left(X_{0},X_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\dots,X_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}};\rho\right).

(2.3)

By the consistency result in Proposition
3.3
this set is non-empty with probability tending to one.

In order to properly describe the limit of the profile MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}
, we need to introduce the limiting process of a suitably rescaled version of the log-likelihood function that corresponds to the likelihood function in (
2.2
).
Herefore, we denote by
L_{t}^{z}(Y)
the symmetric local time at point
t
in
z
for a continuous local martingale
Y
, i.e. by Corollary
1.9
in Chapter VI of
Revuz and Yor 1999
,

\displaystyle L_{t}^{z}(Y)=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbbm{1}_{(z-\epsilon,z+\epsilon)}(Y_{s})ds.

(2.4)

Now, we define two independent standard Poisson processes
N
and
N^{\prime}
on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
and an oscillating Brownian motion
X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}
starting in
\rho
with diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{\rho,\alpha,\beta}
(i.e. a process satisfying (
1.1
) with
x_{0}=\rho
). Based on these three processes, we define
\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}=(\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
via

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)&=\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}\left(|z|\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})+\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)N^{\prime}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})}{\alpha^{2}}-\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}\left(|z|\frac{\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})+\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)N\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right).\end{split}

(2.5)

We use the left-continuous version
N^{\prime}(\bullet-)
to ensure that
\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}
is càdlàg. For
z<0
, we may write (
z\geq 0
can be decomposed analogously)

\displaystyle\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle=|z|\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right)L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\left(N^{\prime}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})}{\alpha^{2}}-\right)-\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})}{\alpha^{2}}\right),

which is a process with negative drift plus a compensated Poisson process in case
\alpha\neq\beta
, where the first statement follows from the fact that
x\mapsto x-1-x\log(x)
has a unique maximum in
x=1
and vanishes in this point. While the limiting drift being a multiple of
|z|
is locally of the same structure as that of classical estimators for change points in time, the stochastic terms is given as a two-sided Poisson process rather than a two-sided Brownian motion for our change in the state space. Recall that the profile MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}
is the first component of an element
(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma})
of (
2.3
) based on discrete observations from the process in (
1.1
).

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
(\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{R}}
be the process given in (
2.5
) and
\gamma\in[0,1)
. Then
(\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma})
is
\sqrt{n}
-consistent and

\displaystyle n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}-\rho_{0}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z).

(2.6)

A route of proof is given in three steps in Subsection
3.1
whose details extend over Sections
4
and
5
. Remarkably, the rate of
(\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma})
does not depend on
\gamma
, whereas the rate for
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}
interpolates between
n^{-1}
for
\gamma=1
(infill asymptotics) and
n^{-1/2}
for
\gamma=0
(null-recurrent Markov chain). It follows from Proposition
C.3
in the supplementary material
C
that this rate is indeed minimax. The limiting distribution is the same for all
\gamma
, i.e for all step sizes
\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}
of the observations. It coincides with the limit in Theorem
1.1
in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
for the infill asymptotics which has been derived in the sense of stable convergence for the MLE on the event
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
when
\alpha
and
\beta
are known. This is surprising, since the limit behavior for
\gamma\in[0,1)
depends heuristically on recurrence properties of the observed Markov chain which cannot contribute to the analysis for
\gamma=1
.

As compared to
\gamma=1
, the long-term asymptotics for
\gamma\in[0,1)
of this article require a completely different proof. For the infill asymptotics, we were able to prove the stronger mode of stable convergence that could be traced back to the uniform stochastic convergence of the semimartingale characteristics of the
sequential
log-likelihood process w.r.t. discretized filtrations. For
\gamma\in[0,1)
, however, this argument is not available any longer and the problem of identifying the joint distribution of the random drift and martingale part of the log-likelihood function arises. Our crucial finding is that in the very special case that
(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
is started in the true parameter
X_{0}=\rho_{0}
, the process
(X_{t}-\rho_{0})_{t\geq 0}
possesses a desirable distributional self-similarity. This enables to relate the weaker mode of convergence in distribution back to the problem in infill asymptotics, where this interplay is well-understood. Recall that the limit theorem in the infill asymptotics is restricted to the event
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
, but
\mathbb{P}(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)=1
iff
X_{0}=\rho_{0}
. Indeed, the latter equivalence is well-known if
X
is replaced by a Brownian motion and carries over to OBM by the Dambis–Dubins–Schwarz theorem (cf. Theorem
19.4
in
Kallenberg 2021
). On basis of the strong Markov property, the artificial constallation of starting in
\rho_{0}
is finally overcome by a coupling argument. In order to prove that the increasing number of observations before the coupling time does not affect the limit for
\gamma\in(0,1)
necessiates a quantitative maximal deviation bound for oscillating Brownian local time (cf. Lemma
5.1
).

Statistical implications.

A priori, the result in Theorem
2.1
is not directly applicable for statistical purposes as the limit depends on the unknown parameters
(\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
. The continuity result in the next theorem paves the way for statistical inference.

Theorem 2.2
.

The limit distribution in (
2.6
) is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure and depends continuously on
(\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
in the topology of weak convergence.

A route of proof is given in Subsection
3.2
and the details are provided in Section
6
. It crucially exploits the particular structure of doubly stochastic Poisson process (
2.5
) which is, conditioned on the local time component, a drifted Poissonian martingale.
This theorem justifies to replace the unknown quantities by the triple MLE
(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma})
. For
\kappa\in(0,1)
define
\hat{q}_{\kappa}^{n}
to be the
\kappa
-quantile of

\displaystyle\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma}}(z)

(2.7)

and
q_{\kappa}
the
\kappa
-quantile of
\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)
. By the properties of the limit given in Theorem
2.2
, Lemma
21.2
in
van der Vaart 1998
reveals

\hat{q}_{\kappa}^{n}\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}q_{\kappa}

for any
\kappa\in(0,1)
. In particular, we obtain that

\displaystyle\left[\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}-n^{-\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\hat{q}_{1-\kappa/2}^{n},\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}-n^{-\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\hat{q}_{\kappa/2}^{n}\right]

(2.8)

is an asymptotic
(1-\kappa)
-confidence interval for
\rho_{0}
. Table
1
displays the proportion of samples lying in the
(1-\kappa)
-confidence interval (
2.8
). One can observe that the coverage is already good for small sample sizes
n
, although the quantiles
\hat{q}_{\kappa}^{n}
were estimated on basis of only
2,000
samples of (
2.7
).

n

\kappa=0.05

\kappa=0.01

500

0.946

0.992

1000

0.955

0.995

n

\kappa=0.05

\kappa=0.01

500

0.986

Table 1:
Proportions of samples lying in the
(1-\kappa)
-confidence interval (
2.8
) for different values of
n
when sampling
N=1000
path with parameter
\rho_{0}=x_{0}=0
and
(\alpha,\beta)=(0.5,0.65)
on a grid of size
1/(100n)
. In the left table we set
\gamma=0.3
and in the right one
\gamma=0.8
.

In order to effectively sample the limit and determine its quantiles, we estimated the local time of the OBM appearing in the limit from simulated discrete sample paths. Here, we used the consistent local time estimator given in Section
3.1
of
Mazzonetto 2026
.

3
Preview of the proofs of Theorem
2.1
and Theorem
2.2

In this section we present the proof ideas of the two theorems of Section
2
, where Subsection
3.1
describes the high-level argumens of the proof of Theorem
2.1
in three steps and Subsection
3.2
the route of proof of Theorem
2.2
.

3.1
Proof of Theorem
2.1

The proof is conducted along the following three steps.

Step I.
We assume
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
to be known and analyze the MLE
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
under this assumption. Note that
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})=\rho_{0}+\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\gamma}
, where
\hat{\theta}_{n}^{\gamma}
is a well-defined representative of
\mathrm{Argsup}_{\theta\in\mathbb{R}}\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\theta)
, where

\displaystyle\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\theta)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+\theta,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}}\right).

(3.1)

Our crucial observation is that if in addition the process even starts in the true threshold
\rho_{0}
, the following self-similarity property of the oscillating Brownian motion holds true.

Lemma 3.1
.

Let
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a solution to (
1.1
) with
\rho=\rho_{0}
, starting at
x_{0}=\rho_{0}
. Then for any
c>0

\left(X_{t}-\rho_{0}\right)_{t\geq 0}\ \stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\left(c^{-\frac{1}{2}}\left(X_{ct}-\rho_{0}\right)\right)_{t\geq 0}.

By setting
c=n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}
, this allows to conclude for the
n
-dimensional distributions

\displaystyle\left(X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}-\rho_{0}\right)_{k=0,1,\dots,n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\left(n^{\frac{1-\gamma}{2}}(X_{k/n}-\rho_{0})\right)_{k=0,1,\dots,n}.

(3.2)

From this property and the explicit form of the transition density
p_{t}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)
we can derive the following result (a proof is given in Section
4
)

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
x_{0}=\rho_{0}
and
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
be a corresponding solution to (
1.1
). Then,

\left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\right)\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\ \left(\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+z/n,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n}\right)}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n}\right)}\right)\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}}.

As by definition

n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})-\rho_{0}\right)\in\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\right),

Lemma
3.2
enables us to transfer results derived for infill asymptotics with fixed time horizon to arbitrary equidistant observation schemes with growing time horizon. Note in particular that in case
\gamma=0
the resulting Markov chain is null-recurrent. In particular

\displaystyle n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})-\rho_{0}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)

(3.3)

is then a consequence of Theorem
1.1
in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
. Note that for the stable convergence result in the infill asymptotics, conditioning on the event
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
was crucial, but
\mathbb{P}(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0)=1
in case
X_{0}=\rho_{0}
.
It should be noted that no martingale central limit theorem is available for the normalized log-likelihood process
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{(-(1+\gamma)/2})
. In the case of infill asymptotics, however, stable convergence could be established in place of convergence in distribution, which allowed the problem to be reduced to the convergence of the semimartingale characteristics of the normalized log-likelihood process
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z/n)
. The identification of the limit was possible, since given
L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)
, the limiting drift turned out to be deterministic and thus could be combined with a weak convergence of the martingale part (compare Slutzky’s theorem).

Step II.
Here, we remove the artificial assumption
x_{0}=\rho_{0}
and establish (
3.3
) for observations of an oscillating Brownian motion started in arbitrary
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. This is done by a coupling argument, where the likelihood functions built from different OBMs, one started in
x_{0}
, the other in
\rho_{0}
, are related to each other. To this aim, denote by
X^{x_{0}}
and
X^{\rho_{0}}
two solutions of (
1.1
) with
\sigma_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
starting in
x_{0}
and
\rho_{0}
, respectively. To define our coupling, introduce the stopping time

\displaystyle T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}:=\inf\left\{t\geq 0:X_{t}^{x_{0}}=X_{t}^{\rho_{0}}\right\}.

(3.4)

As
X^{x_{0}}
and
X^{\rho_{0}}
are on natural scale (see Theorem
33.7
in
Kallenberg 2021
) and recurrent, the argument given in the first lines on p.
85
of
Lindvall 1983
yields
\mathbb{P}(T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}<\infty)=1
. We now define the process
\overline{X}^{c}
as

\displaystyle\overline{X}_{t}^{c}=X_{t}^{x_{0}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t\leq T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}\}}+X_{t}^{\rho_{0}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{t>T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}\}},

(3.5)

see Figure
3
for an illustration. By the strong Markov property of the oscillating Brownian motion (see
Keilson and Wellner 1978
and
Itô and McKean 1965
, p.169),
\overline{X}^{c}
is again an OBM started in
x_{0}
, so the distribution of
\overline{X}^{c}
and
X^{x_{0}}
coincide. In particular, the distribution of
n^{(1+\gamma)/2}(\hat{\rho}_{n}(X^{x_{0}})-\rho_{0})
and
n^{(1+\gamma)/2}(\hat{\rho}_{n}(\overline{X}^{c})-\rho_{0})
coincide and we show that the latter converges weakly to
\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)
. To this aim, we will establish the two following claims:

(1A)

Denote by
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\overline{X}^{c})
the random variable
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2})
from (
3.1
) for the process
\overline{X}^{c}
. Then for any
K>0
, we have

\left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\overline{X}^{c})\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left(\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}

for the process
\ell
given in (
2.5
).

(2A)

The sequence
(n^{(1+\gamma)/2}(\hat{\rho}_{n}(\overline{X}^{c})-\rho_{0}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight.

For
\gamma=0
, (1A) is a consequence of the fact that only finitely many observations occur before the coupling time
T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}
. The proof is conducted in Section
5
. For
\gamma\in(0,1)
, however, the number of observations before the coupling time
T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}
is increasing in
n
, see Figure
3
. Their contribution to the limit is still shown to be negligible but this is significantly harder to prove. Indeed, it relies on a decomposition of the log-likelihood function into a drift and martingale term which have to be treated thouroughly. Additionally, tight quantitative maximal deviation bounds for oscillating Brownian local time are derived for this purpose (Lemma
5.1
).

Fig 3:
Simulation of a path
X^{\rho_{0}}
in black for
\rho_{0}=0
and
X^{x_{0}}
in red with
x_{0}=1
. The coupled process
\overline{X}^{c}
is then given as the blue one. The ticks on the x-axis illustrate the sampling schemes for
\gamma=0
(blue),
\gamma=1/2
(green) and
\gamma=1
(red), compare Figure
2
.

By (1A) and Lemma
3
in Section
16
of
Billingsley 1999
we have

\displaystyle\left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\overline{X}^{c})\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \left(\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)\right)_{z\in\mathbb{R}},

where the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}(\mathbb{R})
is endowed with the topology of Skorohod convergence on compact sets. From the tightness in (2A) and Theorem
3.12
in
Ferger 2015
we then conclude

\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\overline{X}^{c})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\ \underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)

and hence

n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}(X^{x_{0}})-\rho_{0}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}(\overline{X}^{c})-\rho_{0}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\rightarrow}}\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z),

which is (
3.3
) for arbitrary
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. A proof of (1A) and (2A) is deferred to Section
5
.

Step III.
Remove assumtion that
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
are known. As a first step, we derive the rates of convergence of the triple MLE
(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma})
that is given as an element of (
2.3
). Here and subsequently, the subscript
(\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0})
in the probability
\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
indicates that
X
solves (
1.1
) with
\sigma_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\gamma\in[0,1)
. Then
(\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma},\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma})
are
\sqrt{n}
-consistent and
\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}
is
n^{(1+\gamma)/2}
consistent, i.e.

\limsup_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\max\left\{n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}|\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}-\rho_{0}|,\sqrt{n}|\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma}-\alpha_{0}|,\sqrt{n}|\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma}-\beta_{0}|\right\}>K\right)=0.

On the event
\{L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(X)>0\}
, the result continuous to hold for
\gamma=1
.

In the high-frequency setting
\gamma=1
, the
\sqrt{n}
-consistency for estimation of
\alpha_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
was proven for different estimators in
Lejay and Pigato 2018
when the threshold
\rho_{0}
is known to the statistician.
For the proof of Proposition
3.3
, we introduce the normalized log-likelihood function

\displaystyle\ell_{n,\gamma}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+\theta_{\rho},\alpha_{0}+\theta_{\alpha},\beta_{0}+\theta_{\beta}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}\right)

(3.6)

that is the three-parameter analogue to
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha,\beta}(\theta_{\rho})
in (
3.1
) and use that by definition,

\displaystyle\left(n^{\frac{1+\gamma}{2}}(\hat{\rho}_{n}^{\gamma}-\rho_{0}),\sqrt{n}(\hat{\alpha}_{n}^{\gamma}-\alpha_{0}),\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}_{n}^{\gamma}-\beta_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\in\underset{(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})\in\Upsilon_{n}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ \ell_{n,\gamma}\left(n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha}/\sqrt{n},\theta_{\beta}/\sqrt{n}\right),

where
\Upsilon_{n}:=\mathbb{R}\times(-\sqrt{n}\alpha_{0},\infty)\times(-\sqrt{n}\beta_{0},\infty)
. Here, we first prove consistency of the triple MLE for
\gamma\in(0,1]
, which is outlined in Section
7
with details provided in Appendix
F
. By making use of the self-similarity of Lemma
3.1
, the result then transfers to the null-recurrent case
\gamma=0
exactly as in the proof of (2A) given in Section
5
.

Secondly, we denote by
\Pi_{1}:\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
the projection onto the first coordinate. Let
\epsilon>0
, then by Proposition
3.3
there exists
L>0
such that with probability larger than
1-\epsilon
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\Pi_{1}\left(\underset{(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})\in\Upsilon_{n}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ \ell_{n,\gamma}\left(n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha}/\sqrt{n},\theta_{\beta}/\sqrt{n}\right)\right)\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\underset{\theta_{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ \sup_{-L\leq\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}\leq L}\ell_{n,\gamma}\left(n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha}/\sqrt{n},\theta_{\beta}\sqrt{n}\right)\end{split}

(3.7)

This can be seen as follows: By the special structure of
\ell_{n,\gamma}
(as described in Section
2
right after (
2.2
)) the function
\theta_{\rho}\mapsto\sup_{\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}\in[-L,L]}\ell_{n,\gamma}
is càdlàg by Theorem
14.30
of
Aliprantis and
Border 1994
, meaning that the second Argsup is well-defined and coincides with the projection of the first one as the supremum over
\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}\in[-L,L]
is attained. In order to proceed, we observe the decomposition

\displaystyle\ell_{n,\gamma}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})=\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\theta_{\rho})+\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}),

(3.8)

where
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\theta_{\rho})
is given in (
3.1
) and

\displaystyle\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+\theta_{\rho},\alpha_{0}+\theta_{\alpha},\beta_{0}+\theta_{\beta}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+\theta_{\rho},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}\right).

On the right-hand side of (
3.8
), we can add and subtract
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(0,\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})
which obviously does not depend on
\theta_{\rho}
anymore, i.e. arrive at

\ell_{n,\gamma}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})=\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\theta_{\rho})+\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(0,\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})+\ell_{n,\gamma}^{3}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})

for

\ell_{n,\gamma}^{3}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}):=\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})-\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(0,\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})

and then obtain from (
3.7
),

\displaystyle\Pi_{1}\left(\underset{(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})\in\Upsilon_{n}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ \ell_{n,\gamma}\left(n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha}/\sqrt{n},\theta_{\beta}/\sqrt{n}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\underset{\theta_{\rho}\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{Argsup}\ }\ \left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\theta_{\rho})+\sup_{-L\leq\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}\leq L}\ell_{n,\gamma}^{3}(n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha}/\sqrt{n},\theta_{\beta}/\sqrt{n})\right).

This representation motivates the next Proposition which establishes convergence of the function on the left-hand side of which
\mathrm{Argsup}_{\theta_{\rho}}
is taken over towards
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
. Its proof is deferred to Section
E
in the supplementary material. Note that each summand of
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})
is small when
\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
as the density ratio is then approximately one. The main difficulty here is to find a representation of
\ell_{n,\gamma}^{2}(\theta_{\rho},\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta})
for
\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}=\mathcal{O}(n^{-1/2})
that is explicit enough to bound the difference when evaluated in
\theta_{\rho}=0
and
\theta_{\rho}=zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2}
.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\gamma\in[0,1)
. Then for every
K>0
,

\sup_{z\in[-K,K]}\sup_{-L\leq\theta_{\alpha},\theta_{\beta}\leq L}\left|\ell_{n,\gamma}^{3}\left(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\theta_{\alpha}/\sqrt{n},\theta_{\beta}/\sqrt{n}\right)\right|\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}_{0}}0.

Finally, the claim of Theorem
2.1
follows by using Proposition
3.3
as an analogue of (2A) and Proposition
3.4
as an analogue of (1A) and arguing along the lines of Step II.

3.2
Proof of Theorem
2.2

Proving that the limiting distribution in (
2.6
) is absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure requires the observation that the argsup of
\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
is only attained at a jump point of this process. Then, by disintegration, the statement follows from the fact that Poisson processes jump at a given point in time with probability zero.

As (
1.1
) possesses a unique strong solution (cf.
Le Gall 1984
), we assume without loss of generality that all stochastic processes
X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}
are living on the same probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{A},\mathbb{P})
and are driven by the same Brownian motion
W
. A first ingredient to proving continuity in the topology of weak convergence is a stochastic approximation result for the local time
L_{1}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})
.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
(\rho_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
,
(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\beta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
be sequences with
\rho_{n}\rightarrow\rho
,
\alpha_{n}\rightarrow\alpha
and
\beta_{n}\rightarrow\beta
for
n\to\infty
. Then

L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}).

The proof of this result is deferred to Section
6
and is based on Tanaka’s formula. To proceed, we prove the following two items for sequences as in Lemma
3.5
.

(1B)

The sequence
(\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight (where each
\arg\sup
is unique by Lemma
5.1
in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
).

(2B)

For each
K>0
we have

\left(\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left(\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}

in the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}([-K,K])
.

As in Step II of the proof of Theorem
2.1
, (1B) and (2B) together reveal

\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z),

establishing continuity w.r.t the topology of weak convergence.
Although the items (1B) and (2B) resemble (1A) and (2A), they are statements about very different objects: In (1A) and (2A), the rescaled likelihood function
\ell_{n}^{\alpha,\beta}
is treated for fixed parameters
\alpha
and
\beta
, whereas in (1B) and (2B) properties of the sequence of limiting processes
((\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z))_{z\in\mathbb{R}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
indexed in
(\rho_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}},(\alpha_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\beta_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
are discussed. Therefore, their proof is completely different to that outlined in Step II in Subsection
3.1
and uses the specific form of
\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}
as a, conditioned on
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})
, compensated Poisson process with deterministic drift. Exploiting this explicit structure, a slicing argument as in Section
3.2.2
of
van der Vaart and
Wellner 2023
together with Doob’s maximal inequality for martingales yields tightness of the maximizers, given the sequence of local times
(L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded away from zero on a set with high probability. We construct this set using
L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})
for the limits
\rho,\alpha,\beta
of the respective sequence and Lemma
3.5
. Details are provided in Section
6
.

For (2B), we abbreviate
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}:=L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})
and
L_{1}^{\rho}:=L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})
and split

\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)=B^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)+M^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z),

into a drift and martingale part, meaning

\displaystyle B^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}+\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right)|z|L_{1}^{\rho}

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}+\frac{1}{\beta^{2}}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\right)|z|L_{1}^{\rho}

and

\displaystyle M^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle:=\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\left(N^{\prime}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}}{\alpha^{2}}-\right)-\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)\left(N\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}}{\beta^{2}}\right)-\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}}{\beta^{2}}\right).

With Lemma
3.5
it is straightforward to see that

\sup_{z\in[-K,K]}\left|B^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)-B^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)\right|\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0

and (2B) will follow by proving

\displaystyle\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left(M^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}

(3.9)

in the Skorohod space
\mathcal{D}([-K,K])
. As the Poisson processes
N
and
N^{\prime}
are fixed in the definition of
M^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}
, convergence of finite dimensional distributions can be deduced from Lemma
3.5
and by proving uniform integrability of
(L_{1}^{\rho_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
using Tanaka’s formula. Tightness in the Skorohod space is proven by the moment criterion provided in Remark
(13.14)
after Theorem
13.5
in
Billingsley 1999
. This will mainly be a consequence of the special structure of
M^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}
being a compensated Poisson process with random intensity. The details are presented in Section
6
.

4
Details on Part I of the proof of Theorem
2.1

This Section contains the proof of Lemma
3.1
and
3.2
. Both of them will make use of the identities

\displaystyle p_{t}^{\rho+\theta,\alpha,\beta}(x,y)=p_{t}^{\theta,\alpha,\beta}(x-\rho,y-\rho)

(4.1)

and

\displaystyle p_{t}^{\theta,\alpha,\beta}\left(\sqrt{c}x,\sqrt{c}y\right)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}p_{t/c}^{\theta/\sqrt{c},\alpha,\beta}\left(x,y\right),

(4.2)

which are valid for all
c,t,\alpha,\beta>0
and
x,y,\rho,\theta\in\mathbb{R}
and can be read off from the explicit form of the transition density given in (
2.1
).

Denote
Y_{t}=c^{-1/2}(X_{ct}-\rho_{0})
and
Z_{t}=X_{t}-\rho_{0}
. For
A\subset\mathbb{R}
measurable we denote
\sqrt{c}A+\rho_{0}:=\{\sqrt{c}a+\rho_{0}|a\in A\}
. Then we find for
s\leq t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Y_{t}\in A|Y_{s}=y\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}\left(X_{ct}\in\sqrt{c}A+\rho_{0}|X_{cs}=\sqrt{c}y+\rho_{0}\right)

(definition)

\displaystyle=\int_{\sqrt{c}A+\rho_{0}}p_{c(t-s)}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\sqrt{c}y+\rho_{0},z\right)dz

(Markov semigroup)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\sqrt{c}p_{c(t-s)}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\sqrt{c}y+\rho_{0},\sqrt{c}z+\rho_{0}\right)dz

(change of variable)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}\sqrt{c}p_{c(t-s)}^{0,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\sqrt{c}y,\sqrt{c}z\right)dz

(
4.1
)

\displaystyle=\int_{A}p_{t-s}^{0,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(y,z)dz.

(
4.2
)

On the other hand, we obtain the transition density for
Z=(Z_{t})_{t\geq 0}
with similar arguments

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(Z_{t}\in A|Z_{s}=y\right)=\int_{A}p_{t-s}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(y+\rho_{0},z+\rho_{0})dz=\int_{A}p_{t-s}^{0,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(y,z)dz,

such that both transition kernels coincide. As
Y_{0}=Z_{0}=0
, the distributions of both Markov processes coincide.
∎

We have the following (distributional) identities:

\displaystyle\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}-\rho_{0},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}-\rho_{0})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{0,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}-\rho_{0},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}-\rho_{0})}\right)

(
4.1
)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(n^{(1-\gamma)/2}(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0}),n^{(1-\gamma)/2}(X_{k/n}-\rho_{0})\right)}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{0,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(n^{(1-\gamma)/2}(X_{(k-1)/n}-\rho_{0}),n^{(1-\gamma)/2}(X_{k/n}-\rho_{0})\right)}\right)

(
3.2
)

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+z/n}\left(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n}\right)}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}}\left(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n}\right)}\right).

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\eqref{eq_proof:self_similarity_1},\eqref{eq_proof:self_similarity_2}

Along the same lines, this identity can be shown for any finite vector

\left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(z_{1}n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\right),\dots,\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(z_{N}n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}\right)\right)

and
z_{1},\dots,z_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
. As the law of a process is determined by its finite dimensional marginals, the claim of Lemma
3.2
follows.
∎

5
Details on Step II of the proof of Theorem
2.1

As outlined in Step II, the general idea for proving (1A) and (2A) is to reduce the assertion to the case of infill asymptotics via the self-similarity established in Step I. The reason for this is that for such observations the result can be derived using stable convergence for semimartingales which is no longer true if
\gamma\in[0,1)
. For each
z\in[-K,K]
we have the decomposition

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\overline{X}^{c})&=\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}/\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rceil}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c},\overline{X}_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c},\overline{X}_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c})}\right)\\
&\hskip 14.22636pt+\sum_{k=\lceil T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}/\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rceil+1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c},\overline{X}_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c},\overline{X}_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c})}\right),\end{split}

(5.1)

where by the structure of our coupling
\overline{X}^{c}
the second summand is given as

\displaystyle\sum_{k=\lceil T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}/\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rceil+1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}})}\right).

This means that from (
5.1
) we get a decomposition of processes

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\overline{X}^{c})\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}-\left(Y\left(z,(\overline{X}_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}\\
&\hskip 56.9055pt=\left(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},X^{\rho_{0}})\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}-\left(Y\left(z,(X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]},\end{split}

(5.2)

where

Y\left(z,(X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)=\sum_{k=1}^{\lceil T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}/\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rceil}\log\left(\frac{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}{p_{\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}})}\right).

The crucial idea for proving (1A) and (2A) is now to start with the decomposition (
5.2
). Here, we apply the self-similarity argument to the process
(\ell_{n,\gamma}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},X^{\rho_{0}}))_{z\in[-K,K]}
, whereas both
Y
-terms are treated as a remainder. For the low-frequency asymptotics
\gamma=0
, this program can directly put into action, since the number
\lceil T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}/\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rceil
in the definition of
Y
is in fact independent of
n
, as well as the time difference of two consecutive
X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}
and
X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}
. These proofs are given below. For
\gamma\in(0,1)
, the proof is technically more involved and combines the self-similarity and coupling argument with techniques developed in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
for the high-frequency setting
\gamma=1
. Whereas the order of numbers of summands is known, the obstacle consists in proving that the summands are (uniformly in
z
) not too large. Here, the exact reasoning is deferred to the proof of (1A) for
\gamma\in(0,1)
in Appendix
C
. It should be noted that the arguments for the high-frequency case
\gamma=1
need to be developed seperately as the coupling argument reveals a bound of stochastic order
n^{-(1-\gamma)/2}
on the remainder
Y
. The intuition behind this rate is that the larger
\gamma
, the more observations are made before the coupling time
T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}
.
The proof of (2A) relies crucially on Lemma
5.1
which together with its proof is given at the end of this section and is of independent interest. Based on this Lemma, the proof of (2A) is given Section
D
.

First, note that for
\gamma=0
we have
\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}=1
. By Step I, in particular (
3.3
), we know that

\left(\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-1/2},X^{\rho_{0}})\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{\longrightarrow}}\left(\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)\right)_{z\in[-K,K]}.

The convergence in (1A) then is a consequence from (
5.2
) if we show

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in[-K,K]}\left|Y\left(z,Z_{k})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)\right|\longrightarrow_{\mathbb{P}}0\qquad\textrm{ for }Z=\overline{X}^{c},X^{\rho_{0}}.

(5.3)

In what follows, we will establish (
5.3
) for
Z=\overline{X}^{c}
and the supremum over
z\in[0,K]
. The supremum over
[-K,0]
can be dealt with analogously and the reasoning is then exactly the same for
Z=X^{\rho_{0}}
. Recall that
T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}
is independent of
n
and satisfies
\mathbb{P}_{0}(T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}<\infty)=1
. Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary and choose a corresponding
T_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbb{P}(T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}\leq T_{0})>1-\epsilon
. Then we have

\displaystyle\left|Y\left(z,(\overline{X}_{k})_{k=0,\dots,n}^{c}\right)\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{T\leq T_{0}\}}\leq\sum_{k=1}^{T_{0}}\left|\log\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c})}{p_{1}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c})}\right)\right|.

(5.4)

We abbreviate
\psi_{n}^{\gamma}=n^{-(1+\gamma)/2}
. When considering the different cases
I_{j,k}^{0,z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}}
,
j=1,\dots,9
, that occur in the likelihood ratio (
3.1
), namely

\displaystyle\begin{split}&I_{1,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}<\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime},X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\},\\
&I_{2,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}<\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\},\\
&I_{3,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}<\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\},\\
&I_{4,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}<\rho_{0}+\theta\},\\
&I_{5,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}<\rho_{0}+\theta,\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\},\\
&I_{6,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}<\rho_{0}+\theta<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\},\\
&I_{7,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\},\\
&I_{8,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta^{\prime}<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\leq\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\},\\
&I_{9,k}^{\theta^{\prime},\theta}:=\{\rho_{0}+\theta\leq X_{(k-1)\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\rho_{0}+\theta<X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\},\end{split}

(5.5)

one can observe that for
j=2,8
, the logarithmic term in one summand in (
5.4
) behaves approximately like a constant, whereas for all others it is as multiple of
\psi_{n}^{\gamma}
. However, the events for
j=2,8
are unlikely and we exclude them in the following way: as
\overline{X}^{c}
has a transition density with respect to Lebesgue measure, we can find a constant
\kappa>0
small enough such that
\mathbb{P}(E_{\kappa})\geq 1-\epsilon
for the event

E_{\kappa}:=\left\{\overline{X}_{k}^{c}\notin[\rho_{0},\rho_{0}+\kappa]\quad\textrm{ for all }k=0,1,\dots,T_{0}\right\}.

Then, we let
n
be large enough such that
K\psi_{n}^{\gamma}\leq\kappa
, for which the bound (
5.4
) reduces to

\displaystyle\left|Y\left(z,(\overline{X}_{k}^{c})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{T\leq T_{0}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{\kappa}}

\displaystyle\hskip 8.5359pt\leq\sum_{k=1}^{T_{0}}\left|\log\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c})}{p_{1}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c})}\right)\right|\left(\mathbbm{1}_{I_{1,k}^{0,z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}}}+\mathbbm{1}_{I_{3,k}^{0,z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}}}+\mathbbm{1}_{I_{7,k}^{0,z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}}}+\mathbbm{1}_{I_{9,k}^{0,z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}}}\right).

Note that on
E_{\kappa}
the terms for
j=4,5,6
also disappear which can be immediately seen from (
5.5
). This bound is now useful, because for
j=1,3,7,9
we have

\left|\log\left(\frac{p_{1}^{\rho_{0}+zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c})}{p_{1}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c})}\right)\right|\mathbbm{1}_{I_{j,k}^{0,z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}}}\leq z\psi_{n}^{\gamma}G_{j}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\overline{X}_{k-1}^{c},\overline{X}_{k}^{c}),

where for any
a>0
,
\sup_{-a\leq x,y\leq a}G_{j}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(x,y)\leq C_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(a)
for
j=1,3,7,9
and a suitable constant
C_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(a)>0
, see (D.1), (D.7), (D.14) and (D.17) in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
. By the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality, we can choose
a>0
large enough such that
\mathbb{P}(E_{a}^{\prime})>1-\epsilon
for the event

\displaystyle E_{a}^{\prime}:=\left\{-a\leq\overline{X}_{0}^{c},\overline{X}_{1}^{c},\dots,\overline{X}_{\lceil T_{0}\rceil}^{c}\leq a\right\}.

(5.6)

Hence, we finally obtain the bound

\sup_{z\in[0,K]}\left|Y\left(z,(\overline{X}_{k}^{c})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)\right|\mathbbm{1}_{\{T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}\leq T_{0}\}}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{\kappa}}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{a}^{\prime}}\leq C_{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(a)K\psi_{n}^{\gamma}\longrightarrow 0

and (
5.3
) follows since
\mathbb{P}(\{T\leq T_{0}\}\cap E_{\kappa}\cap E_{a}^{\prime})>1-3\epsilon
.
∎

Let
\epsilon>0
. The identity (
5.2
) gives

\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-1/2},\overline{X}^{c})=\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-1/2},X^{\rho_{0}})+Y(n,z,T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}})

with

Y(n,z,T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}})=Y\left(z,\overline{X}_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{c})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right)-Y\left(z,X_{k\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{\rho_{0}})_{k=0,\dots,n}\right).

By the explicit form of the transition density in (
2.1
), there exist constants
c_{1}=c_{1}(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},a,K)
and
c_{2}=c_{2}(\alpha_{0},\beta_{0},a,K)
such that for any
x,y\in[-a,a]
and
z\in[-K,K]
,

c_{1}\leq p_{1}^{zn^{-(1+\gamma)/2},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(x,y)\leq c_{2}.

As a consequence,

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{E_{a}^{\prime}}\mathbbm{1}_{\{T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}\leq T_{0}\}}\left|Y(n,z,T)\right|\leq T_{0}\log(c_{2}/c_{1}),

where
E_{a}^{\prime}
is defined in (
5.6
). We assume that
a
and
T_{0}
are chosen as in the proof of (1A) to ensure
\mathbb{P}(\{T_{\rho_{0}}^{x_{0}}\leq T_{0}\}\cap E_{a}^{\prime})>1-2\epsilon
. Using
\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(0)=0
and
n^{1/2}(\hat{\rho}_{n}-\rho_{0})\in\mathrm{Argsup}_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-1/2})
by definition, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\sqrt{n}\left(\hat{\rho}_{n}(\overline{X}^{c})-\rho_{0}\right)>K\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|z|>K}\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-1/2},\overline{X}^{c})\geq 0\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|z|>K}\ell_{n,0}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(zn^{-1/2},X^{\rho_{0}})\geq-T_{0}\log(c_{2}/c_{1})\right)+2\epsilon,

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{P}\left(\sup_{|z|>K}\overline{\ell}_{n}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z/n,X^{\rho_{0}})\geq-T_{0}\log(c_{2}/c_{1})\right)+2\epsilon,

where the last step uses the self-similarity of Lemma
3.2
and the notation

\overline{\ell}_{n}^{\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(\theta,X)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\log\left(\frac{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0}+\theta,\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n}\right)}{p_{1/n}^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(X_{(k-1)/n},X_{k/n}\right)}\right).

At this point, we reached our goal to transfer the problem into the framework of infill asymptotics. The claim now follows by repeating line-by-line the consistency proof given in Section
3
in
Brutsche and Rohde 2026
from (3.7) onwards, replacing there
0
by
-T_{0}\log(c_{2}/c_{1})
.
∎

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\epsilon>0
and
X
be a solution to (
1.1
) with
X_{0}=x_{0}
. Then there exists
\xi=\xi_{\epsilon}>0
and
\zeta=\zeta_{\epsilon}>0
such that

\liminf_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\zeta\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\rho_{0}+\zeta\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}]}L_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{y}(X)>\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\xi\right)>1-\epsilon.

First, assume
X_{0}=\rho_{0}
. Then, by the self-similarity of Lemma
3.1
and the definition of the local time in (
2.4
), we have for any
c>0
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}L_{1}^{y}(X)&=L_{1}^{y-\rho_{0}}(X-\rho_{0})\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}_{(y-\rho_{0}-\epsilon,y-\rho_{0}+\epsilon)}(X_{t}-\rho_{0})dt\\
&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbbm{1}_{(y-\rho_{0}-\epsilon,y-\rho_{0}+\epsilon)}\big(c^{-1/2}(X_{ct}-\rho_{0})\big)dt\\
&=\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\frac{1}{c}\int_{0}^{c}\mathbbm{1}_{(\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0}-\epsilon),\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0}+\epsilon))}\big((X_{t}-\rho_{0})\big)dt\\
&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}\lim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\frac{1}{2\epsilon}\int_{0}^{c}\mathbbm{1}_{(\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0})-\epsilon,\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0})+\epsilon)}\big((X_{t}-\rho_{0})\big)dt\\
&=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}L_{c}^{\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0})}(X-\rho_{0})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}L_{c}^{\rho_{0}+\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0})}(X).\end{split}

(5.7)

This reasoning also applies to vectors, i.e. for
y_{1},\dots,y_{N}\in\mathbb{R}
we find

\left(L_{1}^{y_{1}}(X),\dots,L_{1}^{Y_{N}}(X)\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}\left(L_{c}^{\rho_{0}+\sqrt{c}(y_{1}-\rho_{0})}(X),\dots,L_{c}^{\rho_{0}+\sqrt{c}(y_{N}-\rho_{0})}(X)\right).

As the process
L_{t}^{\bullet}(X)
is a continuous process for each
t\geq 0
, we therefore obtain the distributional identity

\left(L_{1}^{y}(X)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{c}}L_{c}^{\rho_{0}+\sqrt{c}(y-\rho_{0})}(X)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{R}}.

Setting
c=n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}
, we then find

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\zeta\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\rho_{0}+\zeta\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}]}L_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{y}(X)>\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\xi\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\zeta,\rho_{0}+\zeta]}L_{1}^{y}(Y)>\xi\right)

for an OBM
Y
started in
Y_{0}=\rho_{0}
and with diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}
. Because
L_{1}^{\cdot}(X)
has a continuous version (as
X
is a continuous martingale) and every continuous function on a compact set attains its minimum and maximum, Corollary
29.18
in
Kallenberg 2021
about range and support of continuous local martingales reveals

\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\zeta,\rho_{0}+\zeta]}L_{1}^{y}(Y)>0\ \right|E_{1}\right)=1

for the event
E_{1}=\{\overline{Y}_{1}\geq\rho_{0}+2\zeta,\underline{Y}_{1}\leq\rho_{0}+2\zeta\}
. We now prove that
\mathbb{P}(E_{1})>1-\epsilon/2
for
\zeta>0
small enough. To this aim, define
B_{k}=\{L_{1}^{y}(Y)>0\textrm{ for all }y\in[\rho_{0}-1/k,\rho_{0}+1/k]\}
. Then
B_{k}\subset B_{k+1}
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
and by continuity of measures from below, we have
\lim_{K\to\infty}\mathbb{P}(\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}B_{k})=\mathbb{P}(L_{1}^{\rho_{0}}(Y)>0)=1
. Consequently, for every
\epsilon>0
there exists
K_{\epsilon}
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{k=1}^{K_{\epsilon}}B_{k}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(B_{K_{\epsilon}}\right)>1-\epsilon/2,

and on
B_{K_{\epsilon}}
we have
L_{1}^{y}(Y)>0
for all
y\in[\rho_{0}-1/K_{\epsilon},\rho_{0}+1/K_{\epsilon}]
which by Corollary
29.18
in
Kallenberg 2021
gives
\overline{Y}_{1}\geq\rho_{0}+1/K_{\epsilon}
and
\underline{Y}_{1}\leq\rho_{0}-1/K_{\epsilon}
on
B_{K_{\epsilon}}
. Finally, set
\zeta=K_{\epsilon}^{-1}/2
.
By continuity of measures from below (analogously to the previous argument), we conclude the existence of
\xi>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\zeta,\rho_{0}+\zeta]}L_{1}^{y}(Y)>\xi\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\inf_{y\in[\rho_{0}-\zeta,\rho_{0}+\zeta]}L_{1}^{y}(Y)>\xi\ \right|E_{1}\right)\mathbb{P}(E_{1})>1-\epsilon,

giving the claim for for the case that
X
starts in
\rho_{0}
.

Now, we generalize to
X_{0}=x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
arbitrary. Define the stopping time
\tau_{\rho_{0}}=\inf\{t\geq 0\mid X_{t}=\rho_{0}\}
. By the strong Markov property of
X
, we have

\left(L_{n\Delta_{n}}^{y}(X)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathcal{D}}}{{=}}\left(L_{\tau_{\rho_{0}}}^{y}(X)+L_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}-\tau_{\rho_{0}}}^{y}(Y)\right)_{y\in\mathbb{R}},

where
Y
is an OBM started in
Y_{0}=\rho_{0}
. By null-recurrence of
X
, there exists
T_{0}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(\tau_{\rho_{0}}\geq T_{0})\leq\epsilon/2
and together with non-negativity of the local time, we then find for
I_{n}(\xi,\zeta)=[\rho_{0}-\zeta\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}},\rho_{0}+\zeta\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}]
,

\mathbb{P}_{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}\left(\inf_{y\in I_{n}(\xi,\zeta)}L_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}^{y}(X)>\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\xi\right)\geq\mathbb{P}\left(\inf_{y\in I_{n}(\xi,\zeta)}L_{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}-T_{0}}^{y}(Y)>\sqrt{n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}}\xi\right)-\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

By the argumens used before, the remaining probability can be lower bounded by
1-\epsilon/2
for
\xi
small enough since
n\Delta_{n}^{\gamma}\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

6
Details of the proof of Theorem
2.2

In this section, we will prove both properties of the limiting distribution in Theorem
2.1
, i.e. the distribution of
\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell^{\rho_{0},\alpha_{0},\beta_{0}}(z)
, that are stated in Theorem
2.2
. The existence of a density is shown in Proposition
6.1
and the remaining details of Subsection
3.1
are presented thereafter.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
\rho\in\mathbb{R}
,
\alpha,\beta>0
. Then, the random variable
\arg\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)
admits a density with respect to Lebesgue measure.

Let
y\in\mathbb{R}
be fixed. As the Poisson processes
N,N^{\prime}
and the oscillating Brownian motion
X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}
are independent, we have by disintegration

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}(z)=y\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt=\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\tilde{\ell}^{\alpha,\beta}(cz)=y\ \right|\ L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})=c\right)d\mathbb{P}^{L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})}(dc),

where

\displaystyle\tilde{\ell}^{\alpha,\beta}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathbbm{1}_{\{z<0\}}\left(\frac{\beta^{2}-\alpha^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}|z|+\log\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{2}}\right)N^{\prime}\left(\frac{|z|}{\alpha^{2}}-\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\mathbbm{1}_{\{z\geq 0\}}\left(\frac{\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}\beta^{2}}|z|+\log\left(\frac{\beta^{2}}{\alpha^{2}}\right)N\left(\frac{|z|}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right).

Because
\tilde{\ell}^{\alpha,\beta}
is a piecewise linear function, we either find
N^{\prime}(c|y|/\alpha^{2})-N^{\prime}(c|y|/\alpha^{2}-)\neq 0
or
N(c|y|/\beta^{2})-N(c|y|/\beta^{2}-)\neq 0
for the point
y
at which is the
\mathrm{argsup}
is attained. Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\tilde{\ell}^{\alpha,\beta}(cz)=y\ \right|\ L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})=c\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{P}\left(N^{\prime}\textrm{ jumps at }c|y|/\alpha^{2}\textrm{ or }N\textrm{ jumps at }c|y|/\beta^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(m\textrm{-th jump of }N^{\prime}\textrm{ is at }c|y|/\alpha^{2}\textrm{ or }m\textrm{-th jump of }N^{\prime}\textrm{ is at }c|y|/\beta^{2}\right)=0,

where the last step uses that the time of the
m
-th jump of a Poisson process is gamma distributed and hence has probability zero.
∎

Recall that for fixed Brownian motion
W
,
X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}
is a strong solution solving (
1.1
) with diffusion coefficient
\sigma_{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}
and
x_{0}=\rho_{n}
. First, we introduce the event

E_{n}:=\left\{\frac{1}{2}L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})\leq L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})\leq\frac{3}{2}L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})\right\}

and abbreviate
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}:=L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}),L_{1}^{\rho}:=L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})
in the following. Let
\epsilon>0
be arbitrary. As
X_{0}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}=\rho
, Corollary
29.18
in
Kallenberg 2021
gives
\mathbb{P}(L_{1}^{\rho}>0)=1
and by continuity of measures, there exists a constant
\kappa_{\epsilon}>0
such that
\mathbb{P}(L_{1}^{\rho}>\kappa_{\epsilon})\geq 1-\epsilon
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c})\leq\mathbb{P}(E_{n}^{c},L_{1}^{\rho}>\kappa_{\epsilon})+\epsilon\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left|L_{1}^{\rho}-L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right|>\frac{\kappa_{\epsilon}}{2}\right)+\epsilon

and by Lemma
3.5
, disintegration and Fatou’s lemma,

\displaystyle\limsup_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\right|>K\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\limsup_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left|\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\right|>K,E_{n}\right)+\epsilon

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\limsup_{K\to\infty}\limsup_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\left|\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\right|>K,E_{n}\right|L_{1}^{\rho}\right)\right]+\epsilon.

We now show that the integrand vanishes by proving this for
\mathrm{argsup}_{z\geq 0}
. The general case then follows by an analogous reasoning for
z<0
and the union bound. For
M=\log_{2}(K)
, using
\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(0)=0
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\left|\underset{z\in\mathbb{R}}{\arg\sup\ }\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z)\right|>K,E_{n}\right|L_{1}^{\rho}\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt\leq\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)\geq 0,E_{n}\right|L_{1}^{\rho}\right)\\
&\hskip 28.45274pt=\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)\geq 0\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}},L_{1}^{\rho}\right)\right|L_{1}^{\rho}\right].\end{split}

(6.1)

Here, the last step uses the tower property of conditional expectation and measurability of
E_{n}
with respect to the
\sigma
-algebra generated by
L_{1}^{\rho}
and
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}
. Because the Poisson processes
N,N^{\prime}
in the definition of
\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}
are independent of the Brownian motion
W
and hence of
L_{1}^{\rho}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)\geq 0\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}},L_{1}^{\rho}\right)=\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)\geq 0\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right).

In order to evaluate this term, we observe that

\displaystyle\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)

\displaystyle=b_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}|z|+\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)\left(N\left(\frac{zL_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)-\frac{zL_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)

for
z\geq 0
and
b_{\alpha,\beta}=(\alpha^{2}-\beta^{2})/(\alpha\beta)^{2}+\log(\beta^{2}/\alpha^{2})/\beta^{2}
, which is a decomposition into a deterministic drift and a compensated Poisson process given
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}
. Note that
b_{\alpha,\beta}<0
for
\alpha\neq\beta
. To continue, we set
S_{j}=\{2^{j}<z\leq 2^{j+1}\}
and recall that on
E_{n}
we have
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}>L_{1}^{\rho}/2
and
\mathbb{P}(L_{1}^{\rho}>0)=1
. Then, applying the union bound and Doob’s maximal inequality yields

\displaystyle\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)\geq 0\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\sum_{j\geq M}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z\in S_{j}}\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)\left(N\left(\frac{zL_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)-\frac{zL_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)\geq-b_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}2^{j}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{b_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}^{2}(L_{1}^{\rho_{n}})^{2}}\sum_{j\geq M}2^{-2j}\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(N\left(\frac{2^{j+1}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)-\frac{2^{j+1}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt=\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)\frac{1}{b_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}^{2}\beta_{n}^{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}\sum_{j\geq M}2^{-2j}2^{j+1}.

Consequently, the last term in (
6.1
) is bounded as

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\mathbbm{1}_{E_{n}}\mathbb{P}\left(\left.\sup_{z>2^{M}}\ell^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n}\beta_{n}}(z)\geq 0\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}},L_{1}^{\rho}\right)\right|L_{1}^{\rho}\right]\leq\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)\frac{4}{b_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}^{2}\beta_{n}^{2}L_{1}^{\rho}}\sum_{j\geq M}2^{-j}.

As
\alpha_{n}\to\alpha
and
\beta_{n}\to\beta
,

\limsup_{n\to\infty}\left(\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)\frac{4}{b_{\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}^{2}\beta_{n}^{2}L_{1}^{\rho}}\sum_{j\geq M}2^{-j}\right)

converges to zero for
M\to\infty
. Hence, as
M=\log_{2}(K)
, (1B) follows from this and (
6.1
).
∎

By using Tanaka’s formula and Itô’s isometry twice, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right)^{2}\right]&\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[|X_{1}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-\rho_{n}|^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{sgn}((X_{s}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-\rho_{n})-)dX_{s}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}\right)^{2}\right]\\
&\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(X_{2}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})dW_{s}\right)^{2}\right]+2\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}d\langle X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}\rangle_{s}\right]\\
&\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{0}^{1}\sigma_{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}^{2}(X_{2}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})ds\right]+2\max\{\alpha_{n}^{2},\beta_{n}^{2}\}\\
&\leq 4\max\{\alpha_{n}^{2},\beta_{n}^{2}\}.\end{split}

(6.2)

As
\alpha_{n}\rightarrow\alpha
and
\beta_{n}\rightarrow\beta
, we can conlude that
(L_{1}^{\rho_{n}})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
is uniformly integrable. We now define the compensated Poisson process

\overline{N}(t)=N_{t}-t

Then, because the Poisson processes
N
and
N^{\prime}
are fixed in the definition of
M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}
, by the above uniform integrability and Lemma
3.5
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\overline{N}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)-\overline{N}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right|\right]

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(\overline{N}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)-\overline{N}\left(\frac{|z|L_{1}^{\rho}}{\beta^{2}}\right)\right)^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}},L_{1}^{\rho}\right]\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle=|z|\mathbb{E}\left[\left|\frac{L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}-\frac{L_{1}^{\rho}}{\beta^{2}}\right|\right]^{\frac{1}{2}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle n\to\infty}}{{\longrightarrow}}0.

A similar argument works for
N^{\prime}
such that convergence of finite dimensional distributions in (
3.9
) follows, i.e.
(13.11)
in Theorem
13.5
in
Billingsley 1999
. By the same reasoning, condition
(13.12)
of this result also holds true. Tightness in the Skorohod space then follows by additionally checking the moment condition in
(13.14)
in the same reference, i.e. we prove for
z_{1}\leq z_{2}\leq z_{3}

\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{1})-M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{2})\right)^{2}\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{2})-M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{3})\right)^{2}\right]\leq C|z_{1}-z_{3}|^{2}.

Once this is done, (
3.9
) follows by Theorem
13.5
in
Billingsley 1999
. In case
0<z_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{1})-M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{2})\right)^{2}\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{2})-M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{3})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\overline{N}(z_{1}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})-\overline{N}(z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})\right)^{2}\left(\overline{N}(z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})-\overline{N}(z_{3}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{4}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(\overline{N}(z_{1}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})-\overline{N}(z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})\right)^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt\left.\cdot\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(\overline{N}(z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})-\overline{N}(z_{3}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})\right)^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{4}\beta_{n}^{-4}(z_{2}-z_{1})(z_{3}-z_{2})\mathbb{E}\left[\left(L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right)^{2}\right]\leq C(\rho,\alpha,\beta)|z_{3}-z_{1}|^{2},

where the second step uses conditional independence of the increments given
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}
, the third one that
N
is a Poisson process given
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}
and the last estimate is valid sind
\alpha_{n}\to\alpha
,
\beta_{n}\to\beta
and
\rho_{n}\to\rho
together with (
6.2
)
Now assume
z_{1}<0
and
0\leq z_{2}\leq z_{3}
. Then using
\overline{N}(0)=\overline{N}^{\prime}(0)=0
and independence of
N,N^{\prime}
given
L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{1})-M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{2})\right)^{2}\left(M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{2})-M^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}(z_{3})\right)^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{n}^{2}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\overline{N}^{\prime}(z_{1}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\alpha_{n}^{-2})^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right]+\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\overline{N}(z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right]\right)\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\left.\cdot\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left.\left(\overline{N}(z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})-\overline{N}(z_{3}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2})\right)^{2}\right|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\log\left(\frac{\alpha_{n}^{2}}{\beta_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}|z_{1}|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\alpha_{n}^{-2}+\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}z_{2}L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2}\right)\log\left(\frac{\beta_{n}^{2}}{\alpha_{n}^{2}}\right)^{2}(z_{3}-z_{2})L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}\beta_{n}^{-2}\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 5.69046pt\leq C(\rho,\alpha,\beta)|z_{3}-z_{1}|^{2}.

Here, the last step works the same as before and uses
|z_{1}|,|z_{2}|\leq|z_{3}-z_{1}|
. All other cases, i.e.
z_{1},z_{2}<0,z_{3}\geq 0
and
z_{3}<0
follow analogously to the two cases considered.
∎

By Tanaka’s formula and the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\left|L_{1}^{\rho_{n}}(X^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}})-L_{1}^{\rho}(X^{\rho,\alpha,\beta})\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 14.22636pt\leq\left|\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{sgn}((X_{s}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-\rho_{n})-)dX_{s}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-\int_{0}^{1}\mathrm{sgn}((X_{s}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}-\rho)dX_{s}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}\right|

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt+\left||X_{1}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-\rho_{n}|-|X_{1}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}-\rho|\right|.

The second summand can be bounded as

\left||X_{1}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-\rho_{n}|-|X_{1}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}-\rho|\right|\leq\left|X_{1}^{\rho_{n},\alpha_{n},\beta_{n}}-X_{1}^{\rho,\alpha,\beta}\right|+\left|\rho_{n}-\rho\right|.
